It just hits me the second I walk in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything quite like that. It's really just the elevators and small rooms that get to me. When I'm in one of those, I just feel like I need to get out, but I wouldn't say it builds up to a peak or anything like what you're describing. It's just a strong urge to leave.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you afraid of elevators and small rooms in general, or is it only when you are in them that you feel the need to escape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, but my mind treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I don't sit at home worrying about elevators or anything like that. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being actually locked up somewhere. I know that. But... my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. That's when that feeling hits me.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to escape when in elevators or small rooms because you fear something bad will happen to you, like running out of air, or is it more about not being able to get out if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about running out of air or... or thinking something terrible is going to happen to me. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. Just knowing the door is shut and I can't just walk away... that's what gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being in elevators or small rooms because you might not be able to get out when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it's really just that. I just don't like being stuck where I can't leave on my own terms. That's the whole thing right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being trapped or unable to escape in other situations besides elevators and small rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have any trouble riding in a car, or going to a crowded store or anything like that. It's just those specific little rooms and the elevators. That's really the only time it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a small room if you knew for certain you could get out of it whenever you wanted?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I guess... well, if the door was propped open or I knew for an absolute fact I could just push it and walk right out, maybe it wouldn't bother me. But I haven't really tested it out, so it's hard to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety about being trapped only happens in elevators and small rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, it's really only the elevators and those little rooms. I can't think of any other situations that give me that trapped feeling. It's pretty much just those.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if you were in a large elevator or a big room with no windows?\n\nPatient: I don't really know for sure. I haven't been in a large elevator in a long time. I guess a big room without windows might still bother me a little, just knowing I'm closed in, but maybe not as bad as a small one. It's hard to say without actually being in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious in a small room if you knew you could leave whenever you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, I think you actually just asked me that a minute ago. But like I said, I'm really not sure. If I knew I could just walk right out, it might be alright. But without actually trying it, I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety in a small room decrease if there were two or more doors to exit from?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that either. I haven't been in a small room with two doors that I can think of. I guess having another way out might help, but honestly, I just don't know unless I was standing in one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.230, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... whenever I step into a slow elevator, or, say, a small locked room, my chest just jumps right away. I get this really strong need to get back out before I can even think straight. It just hits me the second I walk in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything quite like that. It's really just the elevators and small rooms that get to me. When I'm in one of those, I just feel like I need to get out, but I wouldn't say it builds up to a peak or anything like what you're describing. It's just a strong urge to leave.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of elevators and small rooms in general, or is it only when you are in them that you feel the need to escape?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really just when I'm actually dealing with them. I don't sit at home worrying about elevators or anything like that. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being actually locked up somewhere. I know that. But... my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. That's when that feeling hits me.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to escape when in elevators or small rooms because you fear something bad will happen to you, like running out of air, or is it more about not being able to get out if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about running out of air or... or thinking something terrible is going to happen to me. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. Just knowing the door is shut and I can't just walk away... that's what gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being in elevators or small rooms because you might not be able to get out when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it's really just that. I just don't like being stuck where I can't leave on my own terms. That's the whole thing right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being trapped or unable to escape in other situations besides elevators and small rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have any trouble riding in a car, or going to a crowded store or anything like that. It's just those specific little rooms and the elevators. That's really the only time it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a small room if you knew for certain you could get out of it whenever you wanted?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I guess... well, if the door was propped open or I knew for an absolute fact I could just push it and walk right out, maybe it wouldn't bother me. But I haven't really tested it out, so it's hard to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety about being trapped only happens in elevators and small rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, it's really only the elevators and those little rooms. I can't think of any other situations that give me that trapped feeling. It's pretty much just those.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if you were in a large elevator or a big room with no windows?\n\nPatient: I don't really know for sure. I haven't been in a large elevator in a long time. I guess a big room without windows might still bother me a little, just knowing I'm closed in, but maybe not as bad as a small one. It's hard to say without actually being in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious in a small room if you knew you could leave whenever you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, I think you actually just asked me that a minute ago. But like I said, I'm really not sure. If I knew I could just walk right out, it might be alright. But without actually trying it, I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety in a small room decrease if there were two or more doors to exit from?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that either. I haven't been in a small room with two doors that I can think of. I guess having another way out might help, but honestly, I just don't know unless I was standing in one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be less if you were in a small room with a large window that you could see out of?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe. I mean, if I could see outside, it might not feel quite as closed off. But, uh, I haven't really been in a room like that recently to test it out. So I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious in a small room if someone you trust was with you?\n\nPatient: I don't know about that either. I haven't been in a small room with two doors that I can think of. I guess having another way out might help, but honestly, I just don't know unless I was standing in one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be less if you were in a small room with a large window that you could see out of?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe. Having someone I trust in there with me... well, I suppose it might help keep my mind off it a little bit. I just haven't tested it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety in small rooms or elevators is because you fear you won't be able to escape quickly if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about escaping, to be honest. I guess having another way out might help, but honestly, I just don't know unless I was standing in one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be less if you were in a small room with a large window that you could see out of?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe. I mean, if I could see outside, it might not feel quite as closed off. But, uh, I haven't really been in a room like that recently to test it out. So I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious in a small room if someone you trust was with you?\n\nPatient: I don't know about that either. Having someone I trust in there with me... well, I suppose it might help keep my mind off it a little bit. But to be honest with you, it's just another one of those things I'd have to actually try before I could give you a real answer. I just haven't tested it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety in small rooms or elevators is because you fear you won't be able to escape quickly if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about escaping, to be honest. I just know that when the doors close in an elevator or I'm in a tight room, I start feeling uncomfortable. I haven't really thought about whether it's because I can't get out fast enough... it's just the feeling of being in that small space that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be present if you were in a wide open space, but still unable to leave?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really been in a situation like that, so I can't say for certain. I just know that small spaces are what really get to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.230, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... whenever I step into a slow elevator, or, say, a small locked room, my chest just jumps right away. I get this really strong need to get back out before I can even think straight. It just hits me the second I walk in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything quite like that. It's really just the elevators and small rooms that get to me. When I'm in one of those, I just feel like I need to get out, but I wouldn't say it builds up to a peak or anything like what you're describing. It's just a strong urge to leave.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of elevators and small rooms in general, or is it only when you are in them that you feel the need to escape?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really just when I'm actually dealing with them. I don't sit at home worrying about elevators or anything like that. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being actually locked up somewhere. I know that. But... my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. That's when that feeling hits me.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to escape when in elevators or small rooms because you fear something bad will happen to you, like running out of air, or is it more about not being able to get out if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about running out of air or... or thinking something terrible is going to happen to me. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. Just knowing the door is shut and I can't just walk away... that's what gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being in elevators or small rooms because you might not be able to get out when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it's really just that. I just don't like being stuck where I can't leave on my own terms. That's the whole thing right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being trapped or unable to escape in other situations besides elevators and small rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have any trouble riding in a car, or going to a crowded store or anything like that. It's just those specific little rooms and the elevators. That's really the only time it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a small room if you knew for certain you could get out of it whenever you wanted?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I guess... well, if the door was propped open or I knew for an absolute fact I could just push it and walk right out, maybe it wouldn't bother me. But I haven't really tested it out, so it's hard to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety about being trapped only happens in elevators and small rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, it's really only the elevators and those little rooms. I haven't been in a large elevator in a long time. I guess a big room without windows might still bother me a little, just knowing I'm closed in, but maybe not as bad as a small one. I know that. But... my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. I don't have any trouble riding in a car, or going to a crowded store or anything like that. It's just those specific little rooms and the elevators. That's when that feeling hits me.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to escape when in elevators or small rooms because you fear something bad will happen to you, like running out of air, or is it more about not being able to get out if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about running out of air or... or thinking something terrible is going to happen to me. It's just... I just don't like the feeling that I can't get out right that second if I want to. Just knowing the door is shut and I can't just walk away... that's what gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being in elevators or small rooms because you might not be able to get out when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was just saying, it's really just that. I just don't like being stuck where I can't leave on my own terms. That's the whole thing right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being trapped or unable to escape in other situations besides elevators and small rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't have any trouble riding in a car, or going to a crowded store or anything like that. It's just those specific little rooms and the elevators. That's really the only time it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a small room if you knew for certain you could get out of it whenever you wanted?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I guess... well, if the door was propped open or I knew for an absolute fact I could just push it and walk right out, maybe it wouldn't bother me. But I haven't really tested it out, so it's hard to say for certain.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety about being trapped only happens in elevators and small rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, it's really only the elevators and those little rooms. I can't think of any other situations that give me that trapped feeling. It's pretty much just those.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if you were in a large elevator or a big room with no windows?\n\nPatient: I don't really know for sure. I haven't been in a large elevator in a long time. I guess a big room without windows might still bother me a little, just knowing I'm closed in, but maybe not as bad as a small one. It's hard to say without actually being in that situation.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious in a small room if you knew you could leave whenever you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, I think you actually just asked me that a minute ago. But like I said, I'm really not sure. If I knew I could just walk right out, it might be alright. But without actually trying it, I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety in a small room decrease if there were two or more doors to exit from?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that either. I haven't been in a small room with two doors that I can think of. I guess having another way out might help, but honestly, I just don't know unless I was standing in one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be less if you were in a small room with a large window that you could see out of?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe. I mean, if I could see outside, it might not feel quite as closed off. But, uh, I haven't really been in a room like that recently to test it out. So I couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious in a small room if someone you trust was with you?\n\nPatient: I don't know about that either. Having someone I trust in there with me... well, I suppose it might help keep my mind off it a little bit. But to be honest with you, it's just another one of those things I'd have to actually try before I could give you a real answer. I just haven't tested it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety in small rooms or elevators is because you fear you won't be able to escape quickly if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about escaping, to be honest. I just know that when the doors close in an elevator or I'm in a tight room, I start feeling uncomfortable. I haven't really thought about whether it's because I can't get out fast enough... it's just the feeling of being in that small space that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be present if you were in a wide open space, but still unable to leave?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really been in a situation like that, so I can't say for certain. I just know that small spaces are what really get to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a small room but had control over the door, like being able to open it yourself whenever you wanted?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, maybe it would make a difference if I knew I could just turn the knob and walk out. But again, I haven't really paid attention to that part of it. It's just the small room itself that makes me feel that way, regardless of the door.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious in a small room if you had a key to unlock the door yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess that's pretty much the same as what I was just saying. I really don't know. I haven't ever tested it out with a key or anything like that. \n\nLike I said, it's just the size of the room that bothers me. I don't think holding a key would make the room feel any bigger, so I'd probably still feel just as uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, open-top enclosure, like a small, roofless room?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I don't think I've ever been in a room without a roof before. \n\nUh, if the walls are still close together, it might still bother me, but... honestly, I just don't know for sure since I haven't come across a situation like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious in a large room with no exit?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure about that either. I don't think I've ever been stuck in a big room with no way out. \n\nBut... since it's really just the small spaces that bother me, I imagine a large room wouldn't be as bad. I just haven't been in that kind of situation to know for a fact.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
